  wall time is that deployment plus the titles' own work; the run-2 outlier is the state where the 48 titles each
  retried a hop that never settled (5 attempts apiece), which is why a wall can cost minutes rather than seconds.
  Every failed title still costs its own attempt budget, so a ladder run's duration is a rough proxy for how far the
  fixture gets, not just for the count. The cold project compile that precedes a ladder is 13-30 s warm-cached
  (`{SCRATCH}/ladder/compile-round*.log`); the focused per-call suites are ~15 s (`solidc`) and ~40-75 s
  (`solidcash`, whole directory).

### 4.6 Rules

- [ ] **Order before the ladder** (the user's I2b decision, made operational on 2026-09-27; superseded by the
  fixture-first rule below on 2026-09-28, which is what rounds follow until the fixture title is green):
  - a round takes the first unticked item of I1, then I2 (Y1-Y7, G5, G3), then I2b (MS1-MS10), and its Log row names
    that item and the `MARKETPLACE_SHAPES.md` row it moved;
  - the marketplace project is compiled at most once per milestone, to refresh the catalog; no title and no fixture
    runs, and a Log row carries no ladder paragraph;
  - a toy or replica settles in its own shape. A narrower variant (a loop for separate call statements, a scalar
    rewrite for an array append, fewer turns, no events) is logged as partial and leaves the toy's box and the
    catalog row's status unchanged.
- [x] **Fixture-first while the roots are compiler-bound** (coordinator decision, 2026-09-28; the order returns to
  I2b with the fixture parked at its measured wall - see the note at the end of this bullet). Rounds took the next
  wall the real project reported - the project compile's first failure, then the fixture's own - ahead of the
  unlanded per-shape replicas, and it earned its place: three real defects (the multi-entry value slot, the reserved
  slot anchored on a body constant, the head's call count against refused entries) came out of compiling the real
  project and none of them would have come out of a replica. Its terms:
  - each wall is first reduced to a small neutral test case in the focused suites, and the fix is what the Log row
    cites; today's three defects all came from compiling the real project, which is why the order is taken;
  - the project is compiled and one fixture title run per round, both captured in `{SCRATCH}`, and the Log row
    carries the rung the fixture reached, with the run's per-test and full-run times recorded in
    `docs/per-call/probes/LADDER_TIMES.md` and summarised in 4.5 (user direction 2026-09-29);
  - I2 and I2b stay unticked until their own criteria hold (their boxes are the criteria, not the schedule), and
    each round leaves a slot for an open security item - T25, T36 and T16 today - until its test exists.
  - **Parked on 2026-09-28 (user direction: prioritise I2b hard).** The fixture's rung is recorded where it stands -
    the project compiles, the Unicrow suite deploys, and `upgrades.deployProxy(Marketplace, ...)` fails with the
    implementation's `initialize` hop refusing a delegate frame at opcode 105 - and the order is back to the plan's
    own: I2b's replicas and primitives first, the fixture resumed when they hold. Compiling the real project stays
    the way a wall is found, and each wall still gets its small neutral case before it is fixed.

- **Claims: work a second worker holds, so a round does not pick it up** (user direction, 2026-09-28: two
  workers in parallel, each in its own scratch copy, landing one commit per item). A claimed item is skipped when
  a round takes the next unticked item; the commit that lands it removes its line here.
  - **The parallel worker's earlier items, done and released** (its active claims are the bullet below). **Done**:
    `MarketplaceV1.takeJob`'s keccak path on the composer side (`447ab2e01`, `55b96c95b`, `101cb6131`), the EVM
    projection's keccak side-car recreating at its own index (`fc52c36aa`), and a delegate frame's side-cars read
    after its own input (`0a0a3d1e7`). **Released**: a keccak256 compute side-car in a per-call constructor
    (`EACCToken`'s EIP-712 constructor): the fixture's compile does not build the token, and the other half of that
    contract's refusal, inline assembly, is this round's per-body admission rule. Taking it up needs the constructor
    guard in `support.ts` narrowed to a loop and the genesis co-spend in `deploy/genesis.ts`. Also released: the
    send-output item claimed in `c00e1c1c3`, a name for each `OP_DEFINE` body, and the composer's value-list helper.
    **Handed back**: the call path's own use of the placeholder guard (`requireLearnedViews(draft)` after its
    derivation, in `call.ts`); a refused call returns its verdicts rather than throwing, so the guard belongs where
    the call decides that.
  - **Claimed by the parallel worker, from `b5f8845dd` (this round's hand-over, on its own recommendation to start
    the `__raw__` design in parallel):** one item on that wall, disjoint from the compiler work this round
    holds (the per-body inline-assembly admission rule and the static low-level lowering it blocks):
    - **value sends to contracts, at EVM parity (spec 4b, user decision 2026-09-29)**: a value send to a
      contract runs its `receive()` or fails, and the SDK composes it. In four slices, each landing with its
      tests: (1) the recipient, **landed** for a master that settles alone (log row "value reaches a per-call
      contract"): `receive()` dispatched by its selector, the call-value check, the containers' growth covering the
      value, a call site that passes no value, and a user's value send in the SDK; its validator half (V7), which
      lets a frame another master of its contract shares carry value, stays open; (2) the sender: a value send to
      a contract is a `CALL` into `receive()` carrying `value`, sends are paid from the containers, and each
      frame's sends are its own (spec 4b, "A send's payer": today two frames can list one output and both count
      it); (3) `address(this).balance`; (4) the SDK: a value send between contracts composed end to end, the
      recipient's `receive()` found in its artifact or refused by name. Served before this: a send to a user's
      lock (`4b68d71dc`). Files: the per-call head and bodies (`support.ts`, `frame-tail.ts`, the
      generation pass), the validator's V7, and the composer; merged onto the implementer's working tree where
      it holds the same files.
    The commit that lands each item removes its line here. What this round holds and must not be duplicated: the
    admission rule at `per-call/master/support.ts:864` (contract-wide to per-body) and the second
    `LowerStaticLowLevelCalls` placement that resolves the nine refused entries' payloads. **Landed**: the
    value-carrying lowering and the per-body send region (`4b68d71dc`), the call path's compute side-cars
    (`3c8349fac`), the per-body assembly admission (`e3eccbc7a`) and the hop's mixed-family layout (`c191fc22b`).
  - **Claimed by this round, from `c191fc22b`:** the dynamic run's base against a mapping **insert** group beside a
    dynamic value — the run advances by the insert surplus while the group declares the relinked predecessor too, so
    it addresses the created node and fails the chunk covenant's address pin (measured; the update shape settles and
    the deployment path's insert settles). Serving it means the run's base stating the group's declared width, or the
    run addressed by a rule the group's width cannot move. It is the wall the fixture's 50-title group stands on.
  - **Claimed by this round, from `16688ad76`:** the element source of a dynamic-array write in a **non-constructor**
    entry. The artifact publishes `dynamicWriteSources` only for a constructor (`Artifact.ts`), so a hop that must
    size the run of chunk records an entry's append creates has nothing to read and refuses by name — measured on the
    fixture's job-post writer (`writes dynamic "<var>" (<Type>) a value whose element this hop cannot state`). The
    work is the publication in the same vocabulary the constructor's sources use, plus the hop's consumption of it.
- [ ] **Limit profile for the gates** (user decision 2026-09-27, applying D3). I1, I2, I2b and every focused suite
  that evaluates a composition gate on the composition profile: `COMPOSITION_LIMITS` in
  `packages/solidc/test/per-call/machine.ts` (relaxed input bytes and operation-cost density, the profile a
  composition harness publishes for its mock network; the suites moved to it in `1bc308187`). The marketplace
  harness keeps its own relaxed profile (`solidcash.config.ts`).
  - Real-chain limits (10,000 B per input, 800 operation-cost units per byte, 128 B commitments, 100 KB relay) are
    measured, not gated: the published-density case in `validator/script.test.ts` reports each input's program and
    unlocking bytes and its operation cost against the published maximum, and Phase R's A9 turns those numbers
    into a gate.
    **That case's first finding is that the shipped validator input is over the 10,000-byte bytecode cap** —
    10,354 bytes of unlocking bytecode against a 10,270-byte program after the V6 order landed (10,568 and 10,484
    before it), which the mock's lifted limits hide; the numbers are in 4.5. Spec section 24 resolves the size half
    — a validator may span several inputs, a head and facets at fixed positions — so the cap is met by splitting the
    validator rather than by shrinking it, and that is Phase R's A9.
  - Until M2 no round spends effort fitting a program under a real-chain limit. A size or density optimization found
    on the way is recorded for Phase R instead.
- [ ] Per-title time budget of 1 day. When it is exceeded, write a reduced neutral reproducer and move on.
- [ ] One full run per day at most. Single-title runs otherwise.
- [ ] Acceptance (Track P4): the new path builds no `OP_RETURN` outputs, no bare `OP_1` outputs, no dummy funders
  and no UTXOs created on demand.
- [ ] Track P rules (P0) hold for all new code.

### 4.7 Deferred: specified or listed, not needed for the 66

These are tracked here so nothing drops silently. Each needs its own decision before it is scheduled.
- [ ] Satoshi and token containers, and payable `ROOT` calls; value accounting beyond preservation (spec open point
  10).
- [ ] `split` / `merge` admin functions for pools.
- [ ] `CREATE` frames inside a composed transaction (spec R3, open point 14).
- [ ] Code-split contracts: facets and `INTERNAL` frames across facets (spec section 11, open point 11).
- [ ] Call receipts (D8, spec 14.1), which would close T32.
- [ ] Users other than P2PKH as callers (spec open point 8).
- [ ] Real-chain limits: validator size and density (open point 7; its size half is resolved by design, spec
  section 24, and built in Phase R), 10,000-byte masters (11), events above 128 bytes (16), and the 100,000 B relay
  limit with one code copy per entry (spec 12, A9).
  - Measured on the reference composition at the published density before and after the program's padding block:
    the budget is 800 operation-cost units per byte of the validator input's own unlocking bytecode, and the shipped
    program spent 3,142,116 of the 3,812,000 those bytes buy — 17% of headroom — and 3,810,464 of 3,810,400 on the
    emitting round trip's shape, 64 over, so that transaction was unspendable while the validator's own derivation
    accepted all eleven checks. The program now carries a block of inert bytes it pushes and drops where it ends
    (`7c76dcef4`): 3,201,534 of 5,452,000, 41% of headroom, for 28,698 extra bytes pushed. The padding is a fixed
    block, so what still comes before a larger shape is the bytes that make it up: a composition whose own program
    and transcript approach the ten-thousand-byte limit on an unlocking bytecode needs a leaner walk — each covered
    master's unlocking bytecode read once rather than once per check, since the reads are what the pushed bytes are.
- [ ] External audit of `V_TEMPLATE`, with B14's reference model, before the v1 freeze binds at the first real-chain
  deployment (D3, D9). Owner: the project owner, who engages the auditor; scheduled in Phase R.
- [x] The address form, decided before the freeze binds: `addrV(cat)`, final for v1, no dispatcher (user decision
  2026-09-25, D9). The validator-enforced receipts (D8) can still be added to `V_TEMPLATE` until the freeze binds.
- **Precondition for any real-chain deployment:** the two items above are done, and the audit covers the decided
  address form.
- **Not due within this goal.** D3 keeps real-chain deployment after M2, so the freeze, the audit and D8's receipt
  deadline do not come due before M2. They come due in Phase R, before the first real-chain deployment.
- [ ] A layout-compatibility rule for proxy upgrades: the implementation's records by name and type, with `__gap`
  carrying no record (spec 20).
- [ ] Atomic proxy initialization (audit F11, spec open point 5).
- [ ] Today's-path security fixes, Track S S1-S3 (section 3b).

## 5. Phase 3: delete and consolidate (after M2)

- [ ] Remove route synthesis (`__message_bus1`, `SCB1`), the row loop, and the `__internal__graph*` bands.
- [ ] Remove static footprint recipes: permutations, cursors, access programs, constructed-key replay,
  loop-domain proofs.
- [ ] Remove the per-shape logic in the layout resolver and the worklist.
- [ ] Reduce the admission catalog to `KERNEL_OPAQUE_EFFECT` and `KERNEL_FOREIGN_DELEGATE`.
- [ ] Retire or re-scope `messageBus`, `messageBusKernel` and `messageBusGraphContext`.
- [ ] Replace `scripts/kernel-beta-manifest.json` with a proof set for the new composition path.
- [ ] Track P4: delete the remaining group C/D machinery, i.e. the `P2SH32(OP_TRUE)` dummy funders and the
  `provider.addUtxo` minting (inventory).
- [ ] Full `yarn test` and `yarn build` green. Docs updated: `message-bus-kernel.md`, `call-model.md`,
  `auto-derivation.md`.

### Phase R: real-chain deployment (after M2, per D3)

- [ ] A9 Size budget, the first step. Owner: the coordinator; about 1 day; its result gives Phase R's estimate.
  Bytes per transaction for `arbitrate` and `approveResult`: masters x (code + witness + arguments + views), plus
  the validator with density padding, state inputs and outputs, from the per-call artifacts' per-function code
  sizes. The recorded result (Log) must state:
  - both layers, with numbers: per input, 10,000 B consensus (code splitting required); per transaction,
    100,000 B relay policy and 1,000,000 B consensus;
  - first, the loader (spec 12, mitigation 1): one copy per distinct code instead of per entry; each master's
    executed-instruction count against its budget of 8 instructions per unlocking byte; the padding needed; and the
    total against 100,000 B;
  - then the other mitigations with their costs: smaller facets (linear at best); direct submission to miners (an
    operational dependency); fewer entries per transaction (gives up atomicity, the design's premise).
- [ ] The validator spans several inputs (spec section 24; user decision 2026-09-28): a head at `addrV(cat)` and
  `K - 1` facets at `head + k`, each running its share of the checks on its own operation-cost budget, the head
  binding each facet's lock and in-place recreation, each facet reading `M` from `OP_INPUTINDEX - k`. A9 sizes `K`
  and the split from the measured program (10,270 B, input 10,354 B at the order walk) and from `arbitrate`; the
  facet hashes join `V_TEMPLATE` before the freeze binds (open point 13). Hostile cases: a facet left out, moved,
  of other code, or reading another head's transcript.
- [ ] The precondition in 4.7 holds before any real-chain deployment.
- [ ] A separate harness at real limits: 10,000 B per input, 800 op-cost per byte, 128 B commitments, 100 KB relay.
  It starts from the published-density measurements (4.6, "Limit profile for the gates"), which become its gate.
- [ ] Facet-split the large handlers: the 75 KB entry closure, and the 90-200 KB contracts.

A callee's call data carries the last parameter's push first and the first parameter's push last: the compiled body reads its parameters off the stack and the item the first parameter names is the one on top, which the measured two-parameter body shows by reading its first parameter at depth 4, immediately above the frame's four values — and the SDK's own unlocking-bytecode comment documents the same order. The compiler folded the digest in parameter order, so the two sides could never agree: with the pushes in wire order the callee accepted while the caller refused, and with them in parameter order the caller accepted while the callee's body misread both. The order is now stated once, in `encodeCallDataArguments` (values named in parameter order, pushes in the order the wire carries them), and the run-time digest folds the values in that same order. Four new cases cover both digest paths — two run-time arguments (a number and bytes) and two compile-time ones — each accepted at the caller, the callee and the validator in wire order, and each refused when the same two values are ordered the way the source names them, which is a call the callee's body would accept but whose own caller refuses. All four fail against the previous order and pass against this one, measured by putting the two emissions and the digest builder back, rebuilding and re-running. The spec's `args` sentence and the canonical module's header both claimed parameter order and are corrected; the composer's plan interface now states which order it takes. |

## Deviations

- **Two of the program's sixty reasons are unreachable in the model's order, and no hostile case can produce them.**
  Every name in `VALIDATOR_PROGRAM_REASONS` is enforced by the shipped program, and the harness's criterion asks each
  to have a hostile case. Counting the names against mentions in the focused tests leaves two without one -
  `V6_MASTER_VISITED_TWICE` and `V9_NO_ROOT` - and probing the reachable tampers says why. A discriminator, measured:
  the reference composition with the transcript's root list emptied (`encodeTranscript({...transcript, roots: []})`)
  is decided `V4_ROOT_MISSING`, not `V9_NO_ROOT`, because V4 asks the transaction's own user inputs first and they
  still carry their frames; `V9_NO_ROOT` needs a composition with no user-entered master at all, which the plan layer
  does not lay out. `V6_MASTER_VISITED_TWICE` needs the derived walk to reach one master through two callers while
  the callee is not itself called twice, and a call counted twice is `V5_MASTER_CALLED_TWICE`, which runs before V6;
  a master listed twice is `V3_DUPLICATE_MASTER` and a root listed twice `V4_DUPLICATE_ROOT`, both earlier still. So
  each of the two is a redundancy the program keeps and the model's order already implies, which is the honest form
  of the criterion's answer for them: not a missing test but an unreachable reason, recorded here rather than
  fabricated as a case.


- **The connectivity walk is greedy: it closes the detached-cycle hole and can refuse a branching chain.** The walk
  follows views and takes what it follows, which is what makes a fabricated pair refusable, but it never backtracks:
  when two advancing declarations start at one view it takes whichever the blob holds first and follows that one, so
  a composition whose executions order the two the other way can be refused though the derivation accepts it (the
  shape the generator reaches when its catalogue is shifted). The old pairing rule accepted such shapes and admitted
  the fabricated pair with them, so the trade is deliberate and is the price of connectivity in a rule that must not
  use the order. The generator's `detached cycle` mutation therefore sits second in the catalogue rather than last:
  appending it shifts the case-to-mutation mapping so the two shapes above surface, and that would fail the generated
  test for a reason that is not the mutation's. **Measured**: with the mutation second the generated corpus is green
  and the differential reports no disagreement; with the pre-rule program restored it reports
  `seed 24301 case 1 (detached cycle): missed V7_CHAIN_BROKEN`. The deletion-continuation half is order-free for the
  same reason and cannot separate a declaration that reads at a view before a deletion from one that reads after it;
  the pairing rule refused that shape too.


- **The gathered blob is ordered by master, not by the executions, and that settles the chains check's shape.**
  `emitCollectRecordEntries` reads the covered masters from the block and, for each, its segments' record entries,
  so the blob's order is (master, segment) rather than the order the frames ran. The derivation chains a record's
  declarations in the executions' order (`assertCompositionSettlement`, which the caller supplies), and a record two
  masters touch is declared by both - in the reference composition, `DA` is declared by `A.f`'s first and second
  segments and by `A.k`, and the blob holds `A.f`'s two declarations next to each other. Measured: a position-following
  walk (one pass, each declaration of the key starting at the view the pass has reached) makes `script.test.ts`
  report 17 spurious refusals, `accepts the reference composition at the validator input` among them, where the
  derivation accepts; the pairing rule reports none. The rule must therefore follow views (`key || view` at the
  record's start window, stride 9/141, width 132) and consume the declaration it follows, which is what the take
  pass does: it returns `[rest, found]` with the first matching record removed and nothing taken twice. The costs
  and the state design recorded below stand; what is corrected here is the order the check reads.

- **The chains check's room, measured rather than guessed, and the two levers.** The design recorded below was costed against the 9,006-byte program: the whole-entry gather is about two hundred and fifty bytes, and the producer-and-consumer check as written — an outer walk over the gathered entries with an inner walk per entry, both computing each entry's variable width — is between eight hundred and twelve hundred, so together they exceed the ~870 bytes the standard ten-thousand-byte unlocking bytecode leaves. Two levers are worth measuring before the check is written. First, the inert padding block can go: it is 1,024 bytes of the program and the reference composition spends 4,386,854 of its 7,304,000-operation budget (60%), so removing it costs about fourteen points of margin and buys 1,024 bytes; that margin is measurable by the published-density test, which is what makes the trade explicit rather than assumed. Second, the gathered entries can be stored padded to a fixed width (a commitment is at most 128 bytes) in a field order chosen so that a producer match is one contiguous run and a consumer match another, which turns each test into a fixed-width `emitFoundWidth` search over the blob instead of a second interpretation of every entry — the blob grows by the padding, which the input pays for in stack bytes, and the check shrinks by more than that. Neither was landed: this round's working room went to the value walk's single read and to this costing.

- **The record chains: the design the next round should implement, and its cost.** V7's last open rule needs each record's declared views to chain across the frames that write it. The derivation groups the record entries by the index they read (`in:<i>`, or `out:<o>` for a record the transaction creates) and compares consecutive `viewOut`/`viewIn` pairs in the order it derived, which the transcript's master list need not follow. The emitted program does not derive that order as data, so the check has to be order-free: for every gathered entry, exactly one producer and one consumer of its `viewIn` and `viewOut` inside its own group, where a producer is another group member's `viewOut` or the spent input's commitment and a consumer is another member's `viewIn` or the output at the entry's own `out`; an entry that deletes (`out == NONE`) must have no member consumer, and an entry whose `viewOut` has no member consumer is a chain end, so its `out` must hold a mutable NFT of the contract with that commitment. The first piece is to gather each record entry *whole* rather than only its two indices — the entry's width is `4 + 4 + len(viewIn) + 4 + len(viewOut)`, reachable from the two length-prefixed commitments the block already writes — and the check then walks that blob, computing each entry's width the same way. Measured room when this was written: the program is 9,006 bytes and the validator input 9,124 of the standard ten-thousand-byte unlocking bytecode, so about eight hundred and seventy bytes are free against an estimated seven hundred and fifty for the two pieces. Both earlier attempts at walks of this shape ended in a program that did not terminate, so the piece to land first is the whole-entry gather with the value floor still green, then the producer-and-consumer check with its hostile case (a middle entry whose view does not chain, and a chain end whose output is not a record of the contract).

- **The record chains are landed, both halves: the pairing, and the chain's end by an anchor.** `emitRequireRecordChains`
  gathers every record entry every covered frame declares into one blob of fixed-width records and weighs each entry
  against the declarations of the same record: what it continues from is looked for as `key || viewIn` in the window
  that pairs a key with a start view, what continues from it as `key || viewOut` in the window that pairs a key with
  an end view, and an entry passes when the transaction's own commitment stands in at an end (`in == NONE` continues
  from nothing; `out == NONE` must not be continued) or when the search finds another declaration's view. The chain's
  end is then decided without the order: each record carries a marker byte, one when the declaration's write lands at
  the transaction's own output (`out != NONE`, `out < txOutputCount`, `pad(viewOut)` equals the output's commitment)
  or when it deletes the record, and an entry passes only if its key has a marked declaration anywhere in the blob.
  That is a necessary condition of the derived rule — the last declaration of a chain anchors at the output, or the
  chain ends with a deletion — and it closes the case the pairing alone accepted (`seed 24301 case 55`: a mutated
  output commitment whose view a read-only declaration also ends at). **Checked both ways**: with the anchor search
  neutered the differential misses exactly that case, and with it the differential is green over the generated
  corpus, so `V7_CHAIN_END_MISMATCH` is back in `VALIDATOR_PROGRAM_REASONS`. This supersedes the room and cost
  bullets around it as measurements: the rule costs 1,018 bytes in total (879 for the pairing, 139 for the anchor),
  the program is 10,024 bytes, the reference composition's validator input 10,107 of the standard ten-thousand-byte
  unlocking bytecode **— 107 over it —** and the composition costs 4,828,196 of 8,113,600 operation-cost units at the
  published density, accepted. The byte budget is D3's and A9's (Phase R), and the reviewer's direction was to land
  the rule as designed without squeezing bytes, so the overflow is recorded here rather than paid for by giving up a
  rule: the prepared-region refactor in the measurements section is what buys it back.
- **The chains rule's cost grows with the square of the entry count, which is the wall the marketplace shapes will
  meet.** The check searches the whole blob three times per entry (the producer, the consumer, and the anchor), so a
  composition with `n` record entries runs on the order of `3n²` whole-record splits. Measured on the reference
  shape: the value-floor program without the chains rule costs 4,386,854 operation-cost units at the published
  density and with it 4,828,196, so four entries — sixteen entry-pairs — cost 441,342, about 27,600 units per entry
  squared. The input buys 8,113,600 units, so the rule as emitted holds to roughly seventeen entries on a shape this
  size; MS7's roughly twenty-four masters, whose segments declare more than that, will need the prepared region the
  measurement section names or a pass that orders the entries by key, and the ladder should expect to pay there
  rather than in bytes. The byte budget binds at the same time — the reference input now sits 107 bytes past the
  standard ten-thousand-byte unlocking bytecode — and the plan's D3 defers that budget to Phase R.
- **The connectivity rule the chains check needs, and why it is a consuming walk rather than a count.** Pairing plus an
  anchor is not connectivity (the Log row and the hostile case in the validator suite carry the repro: two read-only
  declarations of one record given the same fabricated view pair with each other while the record's honest write
  anchors the output). The rule has to establish that a record's declarations form one path from the spent
  commitment — or from nothing, for a record the transaction creates — to the record's output or its deletion.
  Order-free, that is a walk that consumes as it goes: start at the record's start view, repeatedly take the one
  declaration whose start view is the current view (removing it from the working copy), follow the view it ends at,
  stop when the taken declaration anchors at the transaction's output or deletes the record, bound the steps by the
  gathered entry count, and finally require that no declaration of that key remains in the working copy — the
  leftover is what a duplicate or a merge leaves behind, and the bound is what a detached cycle fails to reach the
  anchor within. The walk needs a helper the program does not have: the gather's blob is searched read-only today
  (`emitFoundWindow` returns a count), while the walk must remove the matched record and read its `out` and its end
  view. That is a filter pass — split each record, compare its `key || viewIn` window, park the first match, append
  the others to an accumulator on the alternate stack, return `[rest, foundRecord]` — and with the landed layout
  (`stride 273`) the windows are the ones the check already uses: `key || viewIn` at offset 9 (width 132) and the end
  view at offset 145. Run once per gathered entry, the walk costs on the order of `n²` searches per entry, i.e. `n³`
  for a composition with `n` record entries: the reference shape's five entries fit the relaxed profile the gates use,
  and the larger shapes will need the prepared region the measurements section names. The generator gains a mutation
  for exactly this defect (`detached cycle`: two declarations of one record given the same fabricated view) **in the
  same round as the walk**, because the differential suite compares the machine against the model case by case, so
  the mutation turns every case whose plan carries a repeated record into a required refusal; it is parked with this
  design rather than landed alone, since a red differential is not evidence.
- **The walk's take pass is verified in isolation; its orchestration is written and not landed.** The first half of
  the design above landed as far as it can be checked without the walk: a pass that takes the first matching record
  out of the gathered blob and returns it (`[.., blob, needle]` → `[.., rest, found]`, the parked record below an
  accumulator on the alternate stack). It was verified on a hand-built two-record blob, driven through a P2SH32
  redeem script that pushes the expected results under the arguments and equalifies them: it returns the matching
  record and the rest without it, returns the whole blob with an empty `found` when nothing matches, and leaves the
  main stack balanced. Three defects of the pass were found that way and are worth knowing, because each failed
  silently in the composition: a guard written as `DUP SIZE 0NOTEQUAL IF` leaks one item per iteration (`OP_SIZE`
  alone is the program's idiom, and the leak showed up as a stack two items deeper than the pass's contract); a
  stale work item was dropped along with the needle, so the needle did not survive the first iteration; and the
  window comparison read the work item's tail rather than the needle, because the work item is consumed by the split
  and the needle sits at depth 3 rather than 4. **The per-entry walk is written but not in the branch**: its state is
  `[blob, count, key, work, view]` on the main stack with the body's remainder and an "arrived" flag on the alternate
  stack, the needle is `key || view` compared at offset 9, the taken record's marker is read at offset 4, the next
  view comes from offset 145, and the leftover test searches the key at offset 0 once the walk stops. With that
  written, the validator suite refuses honest compositions — 18 cases report a spurious refusal with `OP_VERIFY` —
  and the cause was not found in this round's working room, so nothing of it is in the branch: a program that
  refuses what the model accepts is worse than the hole it closes, and the hole is at least pinned by a test. What
  the next attempt should take first is a stack-level harness for the walk itself (the isolated harness above is the
  shape) rather than another reading of the emitted bytes: the pass's own defects each looked correct on paper.
- **The key-first gather, as landed, and the correction the first specification needed.** One record is
  `[key:4][marker:1][in:2][out:2][key:4][viewIn pad:128][key:4][viewOut pad:128]`, stride 273: the key, the marker,
  the index pair, then each padded view behind its own copy of the key. The first specification written for this
  round put the second key only once and gave the end window `offset 141`, which does not fit a 132-byte window in a
  269-byte record — the machine said so at once (`OP_SPLIT` with index 132 on a 128-byte item) — so the third key
  copy is what makes both windows contiguous: the window that pairs a key with a start view is `offset 9, width 132`,
  the one that pairs it with an end view `offset 141, width 132`. The marker is `1` when the declaration's write
  lands at the transaction's own output (`out != NONE`, `out < txOutputCount`, and `pad(viewOut)` equals
  `OP_OUTPUTTOKENCOMMITMENT(out)`) or when it deletes the record (`out == NONE`), and `0` otherwise; it is computed
  where the entry is appended, so the gather pays for it once per entry rather than the check paying per entry per
  key. The anchor rule is then one search per entry for `key || 0x01` at `offset 0, width 5`, which compares five
  bytes rather than 132.
- The emitted state-inputs rule is stricter than the oracle in one degenerate place: the oracle keeps the transcript's
  list as a `Set`, so it would accept a transcript naming the same state input twice, while the machine requires each
  listed index to occur exactly once (duplicates could otherwise pad the count). No fixture or generated case carries
  a duplicate, so the two agree on everything tested.
- The emitted rule excludes the validator by `OP_INPUTINDEX` (the input running the program) rather than the oracle's
  `isValidatorInput` predicate. They agree while a transaction carries one validator per category, which every
  fixture and generated case does; a second validator of the same category that is not the active input would be
  counted by the machine and excluded by the oracle. Untested.
- A site the source repeats is resumed by the composer, and the host's progress rule is relaxed only there: for a
  repeated site a stop at the same instruction with a different stack counts as a move. Per-turn call arguments and a
  call result consumed by a later call are still unfillable, the plan states them, and k = 3 turns exceed the
  caller's own operation-cost density (2,184,834 against 2,184,800), which is a wire-form limit.
- Superseded: the census's wire rule is stated in the spec instead of guessed. `OP_OUTPUTTOKENCATEGORY` appends a
  capability byte only for mutable and minting tokens, so an immutable NFT of a settled category and a fungible
  amount of it are the same 32 bytes to the program; the earlier reading (an empty commitment) is wrong, because an
  immutable NFT may carry an empty commitment. The rule is on the amount: every `catX` input and output carries a
  fungible amount of 0, a semi-fungible NFT included (spec S2), and with the amount at zero a bare 32-byte answer is
  a pure immutable NFT, so the census may require it to be a listed event output. The model states the rule and the
  emitted program carries it now (`V7_CENSUS_OWN_FUNGIBLE`: every input's and every output's own-category fungible
  amount must be zero), with three machine cases beside the accepted control — an event NFT at a listed index that
  also carries amount 1, a mutable record output carrying an amount, and a fungible-only own-category output. What
  the program carries both halves now: a bare 32-byte own-category output must be an event some frame in the
  contract's context lists, and a listed event must name an output of the transaction. What the program still does
  not carry: the mutable allowed-list half (`V7_CENSUS_UNEXPECTED_MUTABLE`), the minting recreation
  (`V7_CENSUS_MINTING_NOT_RECREATED`), the event-distinctness rule (`V7_EVENT_OUTPUT_SHARED`), the record chains
  (`V7_CHAIN_*`), the value floor, and the touched-records half of the input rule.
- The master's own census — the one a master runs when it is its category's only master — does not require a zero
  fungible amount on its own-category outputs yet. The validator's rule (above) is what a shared category relies on;
  the single-master case is still the master's, so the amount rule belongs there too (user follow-up, not blocking).
- A refusal the emitter raises on its own is still contract-wide: the per-body rule of C15 is what the assessment
  owns, and a shape the head or the tail fixes — no entry point, a constructor the head cannot share, a fallback,
  contract creation, function pointers, inline assembly — is reported for the unit rather than for a body. Where such
  a shape is one body's, the next step is to move the question into the assessment so the body is refused and
  published by name like the rest.
- The dynamic-value chunk coordinate is a per-body refusal now, and no focused case reproduces the disagreement yet:
  the `.scash` front end and a small `.sol` contract with one dynamic state variable each compile at one width, so
  the shape that splits is the marketplace's multi-variable layout (measured on `MarketplaceDataV1.sol`: its user
  bodies carry widths 3 and 4). The rule's evidence is that project compile and the suites staying green; a neutral
  reproducer is owed and is the first thing the next attempt should take.
- Which convention owns the bus under `composition: 'per-call'` is decided: the **lowering stays, the route does
  not**. The route's synthesis is what lowers the source's own bodies (a low-level call becomes an authenticated
  call, an assembly block is lowered), so it runs; the bodies it adds are the bus's program, so they are dropped from
  the unit before code generation reads it, and the lock invariants that compare a published route's verifier with
  the emitted program are not asked of a per-call artifact. Rejected readings, each measured: skipping the synthesis
  compiles the project only when the route-absence diagnostic is suppressed, and then a low-level call is no longer
  classified as one (the per-body rule for low-level calls stops firing and the body is refused later as an opaque
  external call); keeping that diagnostic instead refuses `MarketplaceV1.sol` for its inline assembly. Skipping the
  route's bodies *at emission* rather than before code generation breaks the kernel's own bookkeeping
  (`KERNEL_INTERNAL_DEFECT: eligible handler '__updateBlockOracle' not in analysis admitted set`). Compiling them
  through the define sub-traversal in the direct convention refuses with "Expected variable
  `__internal__dynamicChunkInputBase` does not exist on the stack", because the per-call path strips the hidden
  frame parameters that convention supplies. Turning the bus off entirely is refused by the source itself
  (`SafeERC20.safePermit` repeats calls to one state owner, `MESSAGE_BUS_REQUIRED`), which is the shape the per-call
  convention exists to serve. What remains on this front is the harness: the fixture's deployment still goes through
  the direct stateful SDK (`Unicrow.deploy` → `deriveDeployCommitments`), which evaluates a per-call master's program
  with the direct convention's frame and fails its own `OP_VERIFY`; pointing that layer at the composer is the D2
  harness item, not a compile wall.
- The harness column of two Log rows read a report that listed both suites twice (`812` is `406`, and the `1174` in
  the row after it is the same slip); both are annotated where they stand, and every count written from this round
  on is a measured run of the three focused suites: `114 + 373 + 408`.
- The category reversal is measured against libauth itself: `OP_UTXOTOKENCATEGORY` pushes `token.category.slice().reverse()` (`bch-2023-tokens.js:213`), and libauth stores the category in display order. So a pin, a reserved category slot and `addrV` are built in the chain's order, and a token is handed to the machine in display order. The master side was affected too: `deployMaster` filled the slot as given and the emitted prologue compared it with the reversed answer, so every real deployment would fail its own category check. The master tests could not see it: all of them use a category of one repeated byte, which reads the same reversed, and one fixture was even chosen around an earlier bug (`fill(0x61)`, an inert `OP_NOP`).
- The whole fix was implemented and verified in one working tree, and reverted rather than landed: the rule in one place (`chainCategory`, used by the validator redeem/address helpers, the emitted pins and the deployment fill), the two pin defects, and a new seeded-category suite (a compiled master spent with the real validator program and a real transcript) that fails before the fix and passes after. The SDK suite passed 228/228 with it, the validator program itself included. What blocked the landing is the legacy master fixtures: they present a stand-in program and a filler transcript, so they need the same real program and transcript, and none of them carries a seeded category. That conversion is the next attempt's first job, and the new suite is saved for it.
- Measured, twice: the machine answers `OP_UTXOTOKENCATEGORY` with the token's category reversed relative to what it is handed, the same reversal the state path applies (`packages/utils/src/state.ts:135`). So a pin and a reserved category slot are built in the chain's order and a token handed to the machine stays in the display order. The master tests cannot see this — their categories are constant-filled, hence symmetric under the reversal — and the validator fixture, whose categories are not, is where it surfaced.
- With that convention applied everywhere, the pinned-pin fixes (the recognition reading the pushed pin, the program comparing its own pin with the token its input holds) still do not pass the compiled-caller suite, so a further check on the head's path is wrong. The next diagnostic has to instrument the caller's own evaluation and read the master program out of the input's unlocking bytecode, because the composition the test builds carries no field for it.
- The per-call tests mix the two byte orders a category id has. The machine's token category and its `OP_UTXOTOKENCATEGORY` answer with the reverse of the id the compiler and the SDK exchange — the reversal the state path already applies (`packages/utils/src/state.ts`, `StatefulContract.ts:5305`) — and the fixture and master tests hand the machine the display order. That is why the pushed pin looked broken: the machine's answer for a fixture token equals the model's category reversed, byte for byte. Every test that fills the reserved category slot or builds a machine token has to speak the chain's order, and then the pushed-pin fixes can be verified; until then the two defects stay unshipped.
- The recognition fix for the pushed pin was written, verified in isolation (a validator presented as `push(pin) || program` is accepted, and one whose UTXO carries no token is refused with a failing pin comparison), and reverted with the rest: the fixture-based master suite still refuses the caller with an `OP_VERIFY` even though that fixture's redeem begins with the pushed pin, its program hash matches the master's reserved slot and its validator input holds a mutable NFT of the contract's category — the same three facts the isolated case uses. So the next look belongs in the head's own composition of the recognition with the tail and frame checks, not in the recognition script.
- The pin in front of the validator program is pushed, but the master's recognition still splits the redeem script at 33 bytes, the unpushed layout, and the program itself never compares its pin with the token its own input holds. Both are real defects and both fail closed; a fix was written (one shared constant for the pushed length, the program comparing its pin with `OP_UTXOTOKENCATEGORY`, and the recognition reading the pushed form) and not shipped, because the comparison it adds fails against the validator suite's own fixture: the machine's `OP_UTXOTOKENCATEGORY` for the input the fixture spends returns a 33-byte value that is neither the model's category for that input (`079b2af8…`) nor the pin the fixture pushed, although the model's inputs are index-ordered and that input's token is a mutable NFT of the contract's category. That has to be understood before the comparison can be trusted, and it may mean the validator suite is spending a different input than it believes.
- The tests that build a validator by hand still assemble the unpushed-pin redeem, so nothing currently spends a compiled master together with the real program: the master suites use a stand-in validator and the validator suite uses the fixture. One test that spends a compiled master beside the real program is the missing link.

- The validator program exists and enforces V1, V9 and the transcript's header and root indices (magic, version, a
  nonzero root count, every root index in range). V2's master-witness and settle-input parsing, V3-V8, V10 and V11 are
  still pure checks with hostile tests, a reference model and a differential harness, and no VM test outside the
  validator suite runs the program yet: the master-side tests still use a small stand-in whose hash the master
  recognises.
- The address derivation pushes the category pin in front of the program: a bare pin is 32 arbitrary bytes and the
  machine executes them as opcodes, so `addrV(cat)` hashes `push(pin(cat)) || V_TEMPLATE`. Every derived address
  moved with the fix, and the identity vectors were regenerated from libauth's own encoders.
- The published operation-cost density (800 per byte) is exhausted by the reference composition before any program
  runs, so the program cannot fit inside it: the validator suite evaluates under the relaxed cost profile, and either
  a smaller program or unlocking padding is required on a real chain (spec section 12).
- The composer executes no scripts: program execution is an injected step function, so derivation, assembly, layout and refusals are exercised while evaluation is not.
- The checklist has no item for the selector dispatch, which every multi-entry contract needs; it was implemented as a prerequisite rather than as a listed unit of work.
- A call's checks are emitted at the call site: the site's own code walks its witness block to the record it names and binds that record's kind, target, calleeRef and argument digest to the source's values, so the digest the code compares against is computed from the code's own operands rather than from the callee's bytes. What is still positional is which record a site addresses: a site's index is fixed by the source, so conditionals and loops over calls remain refused.
- An admitted call takes only compile-time argument values (a literal number, boolean, string or byte string): the caller folds them to a digest at compile time, so a runtime-valued argument is refused by name rather than bound. An admitted call targets an address the source bakes in and is neither conditional nor repeated: the site's code now walks to its own record by position, and a site's position is fixed by the source, so an entry still makes the calls it always makes. A call whose result the caller uses now compiles, and the body uses the record's result.
- Superseded: a call inside a data-dependent loop is admitted for **any** trip count, because the body carries the number of calls it made rather than reading a position the source fixed. The machine accepts zero, one, two and three records against one compiled program, and the published table marks the site `repeated` since it describes the source's calls rather than one execution's witnesses. Two limits stand beside it. A body that reads its own records **and** repeats is refused at emission by name: the block declares one view per segment, and which segment such a read runs in is a run-time value the read does not yet locate — the walk in `declared-view.ts` would need its record skip and its segment skip keyed on the counter, which is the shape it already has for a compile-time count. The composer enumerates sites rather than a repeating execution, so a composition whose loop turns more than once stalls by name at the site whose marker says the table is not an enumeration.
- Superseded: a master's entries may declare different numbers of calls. The head holds the number a block declares and each body pins it to its own source, so one head serves a contract entered as a caller and as a plain state write; the two walks that step over the record section read that number from the block, and a frame declaring a count no body made is refused by the body that runs. What a body may not do is make a number of calls its own paths disagree on.
- A standalone stateful master keeps a prepended pooled-constants prefix ahead of the head; it is stack-neutral, but the head is not literally the script's first bytes for that shape.
- A read-only call site is refused rather than recorded: the emitted kind branch has no static arm.
- A single-entry callee does not check the selector it was entered with; the selector is the caller's to check against the callee's own data.
- Dropping the last push to find a callee's redeem script scans forward by declared length, so its cost grows with the number of pushes before that script. Bounded by the unlocking-bytecode limit, and not measured at scale.
- The record count of each segment is walked rather than required to a compile-time expectation, because which records a segment touches is settlement's business.
- Nothing chains the segment views on chain: the section's structure is checked and the record entries' shapes are checked, but the records a segment computed are not compared with the views it declared.
- No compiled contract produces a send yet: a transfer is refused by name because the recipient lock is a runtime value.
- Event outputs must be strictly increasing, which is stronger than the spec's pairwise-distinct requirement.
- Conditional emits are admitted by the tail shape, which fixes the count, but have no VM case.
- The immutable-output pin's per-call arm is VM-proven for single-entry and dispatched masters, not for a stateful per-call contract, because the records stream is unfinished.
- Records view mode is partial: a standalone master settles a single data-NFT record against its segment views on chain, but the reads still come through the direct covenant at the layout offset the settlement then pins, and a segment declaring two or more records is refused fail-closed until the mapping-node work.
- The cross-segment chain is not emitted: a self-settling master is required to make no external call, so it has exactly one segment and the clause is vacuous for it. That requirement is stronger than the spec's, which lets a contract settle its own records whenever it has one master that made no `DELEGATE` or `INTERNAL` call — such a master may make ordinary calls and then has two or more segments whose chain is its own business. Compiling calls where they happen (now landed) is what unblocks the chain; the validator is what a contract entered more than once needs.
- An attempt at the census exemption got the stack accounting right and the refusal right, and stopped there: the emitted arm still refuses a hand-built validator input, so the failure is inside the recognition's own expectations about a validator's unlocking bytecode (what precedes the redeem script and what the reader accepts there), not in the census. Taking the reserved hash off the alternate stack into the settlement stack and keeping the strict census rule leaves every existing settlement test passing, so that part is sound; the accept case needs the validator presentation built the way the recognition reads it.
- Admitting a validator of the contract's own category into the settlement census needs the validator program's hash at the census point, and the head consumes its reserved slot in the need test. Pushing the slot a second time does not work: a descriptor publishes one offset per slot, so the second push is never filled. The slot has to be kept for the whole head, which is a change to the head's emission order and to the settlement script's use of the alternate stack.
- The standalone settlement census refuses every mutable token of the contract other than the record it declares, so a self-settling master of X in a transaction that also spends a validator of X's own category — which the spec's S2 allows — is refused. The validator's recreated token has to be admitted; latent while a self-settling master can still make no external call.
- A listed send carries no paying record on the wire, so the value floor is emitted only for an entry that lists no send. The spec was reverted to the wire form rather than the codec version-bumped: v1 pays a send from the contract's state record, and the 66 make no BCH send.
- A read-only entry of a per-call contract behind a proxy is refused by name rather than answered: the read path
  derives a frame over one category, and a proxy keeps its records under its own category while the implementation's
  master runs under its own, so the read would have to run the hop's own two-category plan. Calls (which do run it)
  are served; a read through a proxy is not planned yet.
- The harness is pointed at the composition path as of 2026-09-28 (the box above): the testing layer's factory and
  the handle it returns route a per-call artifact by what the artifact publishes, so a project whose config sets
  `composition: 'per-call'` deploys and calls through the composer with no source or test change. The size budget
  stays in Phase R after M2 as decided.
- The cross-segment chain is emitted: a master walks every segment of its execution in order, so the first declaration
  begins at the commitment the transaction spends, each later one carries on from the one before it, and the last ends
  at the commitment the transaction writes, and the census runs once over the whole chain. The chain's registers park
  on the alternate stack between segments. Because the settlement is emitted in the validator arm too, behind a count
  of the contract's masters, a master that makes ordinary calls now settles its own records whenever it is its
  category's only master in the transaction, which the spec's S clause allows and which a single-master callee needs.
  One chain per master: a segment declaring several records, a later segment declaring a record the chain does not
  name, and a segment whose declaration does not chain are refused fail-closed rather than settled partly.
- A compiled body still reads its state as `OP_INPUTINDEX + offset`, so in a composed transaction the record has to sit
  immediately after its master; reading it out of the segment views instead is the remaining gap between a master's
  declaration and the state its body computed. A body whose record sits elsewhere is refused, not silently misread.
- The settlement's own tests are 13 cases that drive the emitted walk on its own over real witness blocks and
  transactions (chained, unchained, empty segments, created and deleted records, an undeclared state UTXO, a value
  loss, a block with a call record) and 7 that drive a compiled stateful master whose execution is split at a call.
- A chain whose first declaration creates its record — the record is not among the transaction's inputs — is refused
  fail-closed: the settlement reads the record's index in the index space inputs and outputs share, so the value floor
  and the census of the first declaration need the same treatment as the chain itself before a created record can be
  read back in a later segment. The plan's shapes create records (a mapping entry written and then used) so this is
  the next thing the chain needs.
- A stateful per-call entry that also emits an event is now proven in the machine: the settlement parks its chain
  registers on the alternate stack, and the event walk parks each listed output index there before the settlement
  runs, so the four new cases fix that coexistence (accepted with the declared record, refused for a declaration the
  transaction does not spend, refused for a payload the body did not compute, refused for a record output the
  declaration does not name). The record sits right after its master, which is where a compiled body reads it from.
- The next unit on the critical path is the state read itself: a compiled body reads its state as
  `OP_INPUTINDEX + offset`, so a composed master's record has to sit immediately after it. The two places to change are
  `emitStateFieldReadInline` (one site, `OP_INPUTINDEX <offset> OP_ADD OP_UTXOTOKENCOMMITMENT` becomes the declared
  record's index) and the state-commitment write where the epilogue serialises the pending writes; the head has to
  leave the declared record's input and output indices as tracked slots for the body to read, the way it already
  leaves each listed event's output index.
- The state read and write do not compute their index ad hoc: most sites go through `emitMasterInputIndex` (which
  already returns a frame's authenticated owner instead of the active input for delegate frames), so a per-call
  implementation of that one helper is where the offset stops. The remaining direct uses of the compact offset are the
  single read in `emitStateFieldReadInline` and the output side of the state-commitment write. The head also has to
  leave the declared record's input and output indices as tracked slots first, because they are what those sites read.
- V7's chain rule does not need a depth-first walk in script after all: a settled contract's records chain in the
  order the covered masters appear in `M.masters`, each master's segments in block order, so a linear scan over that
  list is enough. A wrong order breaks the chain and is refused, so the scan is fail-closed rather than assuming the
  transcript is honest. What is missing is the per-master walk itself: today a validator-settled master's own segment
  declarations are unchecked, because only a self-settling master walks them (the same walk, moved into the validator,
  is what the chain needs first). The cross-master linkage is the second half and needs that walk as its base.
- The state index cannot be moved by changing the settlement alone: the head's frame model is built by the checks
  that run before the settlement, so an item the walk leaves on the stack shifts every pick in them. The settlement
  has to be emitted after the frame values are arranged — in the composed path after the two rotations that put
  `origin` and `sender` on top, and in both arms of the need test with the same item count, because the frame's shape
  must not depend on which arm ran. The body then reads the record's offset from its ordinal in the frame, since
  untracked operands sit above the frame at every state site. An attempt that left the offset where the settlement
  currently runs broke the frame for every per-call master, and was reverted; doing it properly starts with that
  emission order.
- The per-call code generator refuses any compiled unit that has imports (`packages/solidc/src/per-call/master/support.ts:138`), and every marketplace root imports, so the harness pointed at the convention cannot compile the project at all: the ladder's first rung is a compile refusal, not a failing fixture. The guard is a fact about emission order — imported definitions are emitted before the master head — so supporting imports means either emitting the head first and the unit-level definitions after it, or proving each prefix stack-neutral the way the standalone stateful master's pooled-constants prefix already is. Until then the 66 path is blocked before any title can run.

- Superseding the import bullet above in full: the definition prefix is admitted (`1a197c846`). A master's head records how many bytes its unit's definitions occupy and opens after them, which is the stack-neutral prefix the bullet asks for — a definition is a body push, an identifier push and `OP_DEFINE`, executed where it stands. The guard is gone rather than narrowed, because the shapes a definition could carry that the head cannot serve are the shapes the compiler never outlines: an emitting definition, an emitting library definition, and a definition making an external call all compile to programs carrying no definition for them, measured. The unit that reported first behind that wall, `unicrow/UnicrowClaim.sol`, then reported its inline assembly.

- Superseding the assembly clause above at `d9d41484e`: the question is asked of the code the master carries rather than of the unit. A unit is a file and its imports, and one contract's master holds its own bodies and the definitions those bodies reach, so OpenZeppelin's `Address` helpers — imported beside a token that never calls them — no longer refuse the contract next to them. The narrowing is measured both ways: an assembly-bearing definition no body reaches leaves the compiled master byte-identical, and assembly in the entry, or in a definition the entry calls, is refused by name. `unicrow/UnicrowClaim.sol` reports next: "the entry point 'claim' returns an array, but a frame's `retval` is compared with one scalar value the entry computed, which a composite value is not yet" — an entry whose return type is composite, which the frame has no encoding to compare against.

- Superseding the composite-return clause above at `362cb13eb`: a fixed-size array return is admitted. The frame's `retval` compares the blob the entry's own `return` built, and an array is one value the language holds already encoded — the ABI words of its elements under a Solidity import — so the comparison is the one a scalar return makes; a struct, a tuple, a mapping, a function pointer and a token container stay refused, with the struct case beside the array's in the test file naming the boundary. `unicrow/UnicrowClaim.sol` reports next: "the entry point 'claimMultiple' carries the hidden parameter '__internal__externalReturnOutputIdx0', which the master head does not supply" — an operand the direct path threads into a body so its return-event output can be named, which a frame that carries `retval` has no use for, so the admission should recognise it rather than refuse the contract.

The refusal that named a conditional call first was over-broad, not the shape: a call to a stateful callee carries the data-NFT indices the direct convention co-spends its records through, and those are not part of the record a per-call site binds, so they no longer refuse anyone. The site list is likewise flat (`perCallEntryCallSites`, one entry per call in source order) while a path executes one arm: `unicrow/UnicrowClaim.sol`'s `claim` has two sites and a path count of one, which is why the count pin `own = perCallEntryCallSites.get(name).length` (`:3182`) cannot answer for it. - **A record the transaction creates is named by its output index on the frames that rewrite it, and by the create
  sentinel only on the frame that creates it.** The spec's sections 16 and 20 say a created record's entry carries
  `in = 0xFFFF` on every frame. The shipped body matches a record's read on its `in` field, so a later frame that
  reads the record back must name it by the output it appears at (`in == out == that index`), while the creating
  declaration keeps the sentinel. The transcript was extended to list a settled contract's created outputs (wire
  version 2, vectors regenerated) precisely so the program can tell such an index apart from a spent state UTXO: a
  touched-records check, a chain start, the value floor and the chain key all treat a listed created record as a
  record with no input rather than as an input that happens to exist. The spec's sections 6, 16 and 20 have since
  been corrected to this rule, including the wire version byte and the settlement's created-output list. A created index of 128 or more would not match
  the one-byte list the program searches, so the rule fails closed (it demands a state UTXO) instead of accepting
  wrongly; the toy's indices are small. The spec text should be corrected to the shipped rule.

- **A per-call genesis mints the scalar state record at the deployer's lock, while the design says every record
  lock is derived from the contract's category.** Spec section 20 states it plainly ("Every record lock is derived
  from the contract's category `C`, plus a salt where noted", and "every record lock pins its own category and
  capability, so a spent UTXO at a record lock is a genuine record"), and section 3.3 has state UTXOs sitting at
  `stateX`, the data-NFT covenant. The shipped per-call deployment mints that one record at
  `options.funder.generateLockingBytecode()` - a plain user lock - and the focused composition tests build their
  state record at a user lock too, so the two agree with each other and disagree with the design. Two consequences
  follow, and neither is tested: a state NFT at a user lock is spendable by that lock's owner outside the contract's
  rules, and the identity argument that a state UTXO can never act as a caller (T19) rests on the record's lock
  pinning the category, which a user lock does not. The mapping node, chunk and event records do sit at their
  collection covenants. **Reconciled as of `c67d9ec73`**: the genesis mints at the data-NFT covenant, the covenant's
  own unlocking is built by shared helpers (`stateRecordLockingBytecode`, `stateRecordUnlockingBytecode`), and the
  fixtures that mirrored the old choice carry the record at the covenant with the record input's own verdict
  asserted beside the masters'. Recorded as remaining: three edge fixtures (`edges/reentrant-guard`,
  `edges/writes-after-call`, `edges/repeated-callee`) still carry a state record at a plain user lock, and the
  genesis probe's synthetic record inputs are never evaluated (only the master's input is).

On the shipped per-call compiler both forms are **refused per body** instead of lowered: with the result bound the reason is the direct path's return-event machinery, as a bare statement the missing call-witness checks - so no non-canonical argument digest is ever published. Measured on a mixed call, the preimage was 40 bytes where the record's hash is over 41, missing the address's `0x23` length byte; the digest could never match, and the run failed at the call site's own `OP_EQUALVERIFY`, which the driver reported as a stall at the caller's segment close. A call with only compile-time arguments was never affected, because the compiler folds that digest itself through the shipped encoder — which is why the defect needed a mixed list to show. The fix is one branch: leave the push on the stack as data, exactly as the run-time branch's own fragment does. Two pins elsewhere were the same defect and became settles (the re-entry toy whose comment described it, and the appended-root shape whose `append.call(1, a)` mixes a constant with a run-time value). The second was the mapping-node twin of the fix `e973901bd` made for data-NFT records: `emitReadOnlyEntryVerification` closed a read-only entry slot against the transaction's own input/output commitment pair, so a frame reading a node another frame rewrote in the same transaction could never close. A per-call frame now compares through the view its own segment declares. Measured: `Root.run()` → `Store.getFee`, `Token.write(fee)`, `Token.read()` derives, every contract input is accepted, the node output equals the shipped encoder's commitment for the value written, and the read frame's `retval` is that value. The four rungs were one SDK-deployment defect (a per-call `deployProxy` handle whose bare names were the deployment's record rather than the implementation's entries, so `marketplace.initialize(...)` was not a function — `c42b62e99`), the refused-write reason on both surfaces (`84955377b`), the hop's struct-valued mapping write resolved from the entry's own arguments (`31e0f645e`), and the last open security row: a call whose run-time target is the caller's own address now binds its record to the caller's frame `self`, so T25's two spellings agree (`8b6b58bda`). The 48-title group's own refusal is gone — it was run-time, not compile-time, which is why the census stays 12 while its composition changed — and the group now stops on a dynamic `address[]` append the hop does not plan. The next levers, in order: the hop's dynamic-state run, value sources that read prior state, a mapping delete, then the compiler's `__raw__` call path (9 of the 12 refusals) and the two mapping-behind-a-call segment operands. The compiler's publication of an inlined internal helper's `require` message is the one reason-reporting gap left.
